HIGHLIGHTS
 
Introduction
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
                Indicator Compiled
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 The National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) has been conducting Household 
Consumption Expenditure Survey (HCES) since the inception of National 
Sample Survey in the year 1950.  
 Information collected in HCES helps to assess trends in economic well -being 
and to determine and update the basket of consumer goods and services . It is 
also used for the calculation of the weights for Consumer Price Indices. 
 The latest survey (HCES: 2023 -24) has been conducted during the period 
August, 2023 to July, 2024 in which information has been collected from 
2,61,953 Households (1,54,357  in rural areas and 1,07,596  in urban areas) 
spread over all States and Union Territories in the country.  
 Prior to this, a survey of similar scale was conducted during August 2022 to 
July 2023. 
The following indicators have been generated from HCES: 2023-24: 
 Average Monthly Per Capita Consumption Expenditure (MPCE) by; 
 State/UT and All-India; 
 Fractile classes of MPCE; 
 Item category of consumption; 
 Household Type; 
 Social Group 
 Average Monthly Per Capita Quantity Consumption of selected items for; 
 State/UT and All-India; 
 Fractile classes of MPCE; 
 Item category of consumption.

Chapter One 
Introduction 
 
 
1.1 BACKGROUND 
 
1.1.0 The Household Consumption Expenditure Survey (HCES) is designed to collect 
information on consumption and expenditure of the households on goods and services. 
HCES provides data required to assess trends in economic well-being and to determine and 
update the basket of consumer goods and services . It is also used to compute the weights 
for compilation of the Consumer Price Index. Data collected in HCES is a vital input in 
developing measures for  welfare, poverty, inequality  and also for formulating and 
monitoring economic and social welfare policies, especially for different segments of the 
population. Analysis of HCES data over times inform us about how the level of living and 
consumption behaviour of the households change over time, and about how levels of living 
vary among individuals and groups in the economy. Food consumption data compiled from 
HCES are an important source of information on food security and nutrition intake. 
 
1.1.1 In view of the recommendations of the National Statistical Commission (NSC), it was 
planned to undertake the next survey on household consumption expenditure during July, 
2020 to June, 2021. Later, the Ministry of Statistics & Programme Implementation (MoSPI) 
decided to conduct two back -to-back surveys on the subject to facilitate a decision on 
revision of the base year for macro -economic indicators, like Gross Domestic Product 
(GDP), Consumer Price Indices (CPI) etc. However, the emergence of COVID -19 
pandemic delayed the launch of the survey fieldwork. Subsequently, after return of 

PDF_PAGE 20
 
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 2 
normalcy, two consecutive surveys have been planned during the period August , 2022 to 
July, 2023 and August, 2023 to July, 2024. After completion of the fieldwork of the first 
survey, a factsheet has been published by MoSPI in February, 2024 and thereafter, the 
detailed report of the survey along with the unit level data has been released in June, 2024. 
A factsheet containing summary results of HCES:2023-24 has been published in December 
2023. This is the detailed report of HCES: 2023-24. 
 
1.2 PAST SURVEYS 
 
1.2.0 The National Sample Survey Office (N SSO) has been conducting surveys on 
household consumption expenditure since the inception of the National Sample Survey 
(NSS) in the year 1951. Thereafter, surveys on consumption expenditure had been 
conducted at regular intervals as part of NSS rounds, normally of one -year duration. The 
data on household consumption expenditure were collected in every round up to the 28 th 
(1973-74). After the 26 th round (1971-72) of the survey, the then Governing Council of 
NSSO decided to conduct the surveys on consumption expenditure and employment -
unemployment together on a large scale, once in every five years. From the 42 nd round 
(1986-87), on the basis of the recommendation of the Governing Council, NSS had started 
annual surveys of consumption expenditure in a smaller-scale with an abridged version of 
the schedule and thin sample. This annual series was continued till NSS 64 th round (2007-
08).  
 
1.2.1 The quinquennial surveys were conducted and results were published in the 27 th 
(1972-73), 32 nd (1977-78), 38 th (1983), 43 rd (1987-88), 50 th (1993-94), 55 th (1999-‘00), 61 st 
(2004-05), 66th (2009-10) and 68th (2011-12) rounds of NSS, at roughly 5-year intervals. The 
last survey has been conducted in during August, 2022 to July, 2023 (2022-23). The survey 
of 2023-24 is the latest one on the subject.  
 
 

PDF_PAGE 21
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023 -24                              Page 3  
1.3 SURVEY COVERAGE 
 
1.3.1 Geographical Coverage 
 
1.3.1.0 The survey covered the whole of the Indian Union except a few inaccessible villages 
in the Andaman and Nicobar Islands. The survey was conducted in 8,684 villages and 6,143 
urban blocks covering 2,61,953 Households (1,54,357 in rural areas and 1,07,596 in 
urban areas). The distribution of the villages/urban blocks and households surveyed across 
different States/UTs for rural and urban areas is given in Table 1.1 at the end of this Chapter. 
 
1.3.2 Population Coverage 
 
1.3.2.0 All resident households located in the geographical territory of Indian union were 
considered in the survey with the following exclusions:    
 
1.3.2.1 Floating population, i.e., persons without any normal residence, was excluded from 
the coverage of the survey . But persons residing in open space s, roadside shelter s, under 
bridges etc., more or less regularly in the same place were covered. 
 
1.3.2.2 Foreign nationals as well as their domestic servants were excluded, if by definition 
the latter belonged to the foreign national's household. However, a foreign national who 
had become an Indian citizen for all practical purposes was covered.  
 
1.3.2.3 Persons residing in barracks of military and paramilitary forces (like Police, Border 
Security Force, etc.) were kept outside the survey coverage. However, civilian population 
residing in their neighbourhood, including the family quarters of service personnel w as 
covered. 
 
1.3.2.4 Orphanages, rescue homes, ashrams and vagrant houses were outside the survey 
coverage. However, the persons staying in old age homes, the students staying in 
ashram/hostels and the residential staff (other than monks/nuns) of these ashrams were 

PDF_PAGE 22
 
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 4 
covered. Although orphans living in orphanages were excluded, the persons looking after 
them and staying there were covered. Convicted prisoners undergoing sentence were kept 
outside the coverage of the survey. 
 
1.4 QUESTIONNAIRES FOR THE SURVEY 
 
1.4.1 HCES: 2023-24 has been conducted using three questionnaires identical to that of 
HCES:2022-23, namely, (i) Questionnaire: FDQ for Food items, (ii) Questionnaire: CSQ for 
Consumables and Services items, and (iii) Questionnaire: DGQ for Durable goods. These 
three q uestionnaires have been canvassed in the selected households in three separate 
consecutive monthly visits. A slightly modified version of Questionnaire: HCQ as used in 
HCES: 2022-23 after deleting items related to: (i) type of material used for construction of 
wall, floor and roof of the building, (ii) source of drinking water and time taken to fetch 
drinking water from the source, (iii) type of latrine used and type of access of the household 
to latrine, (iv) number of members of the household aged between 0 to 18 years died during 
the past 5 years preceding the date of survey etc. has been canvassed in HCES: 2023-24. 
 
1.4.2 All possible six sequencing of the three questionnaires, namely, [(Q1, Q2, Q3), (Q1, 
Q3, Q2), (Q2, Q1, Q3), (Q2, Q3, Q1), (Q3, Q1, Q2) and (Q3, Q2, Q1)], where Q1 refers to 
FDQ, Q2 refers to CSQ and Q3 refers to DGQ, were used in the survey to eliminate any 
bias due to adoption of any particular sequencing of the questionnaires. The questionnaires 
are provided in Appendix D.  
 
1.5 REFERENCE PERIOD 
 
1.5.0 The consumption of any good or service by members of a household (individually or 
collectively) occurs in the form of a flow over time. The time period for which consumption 
is recorded is called the reference period. It may vary from item to item. As the respondents 
are asked to recall and report the volume of consumption  over a fixed duration , the 
reference period is also called the recall period. 

PDF_PAGE 23
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023 -24                              Page 5  
1.5.1 The reference periods used in the survey for different groups of items are given below: 
Category Item groups Reference 
period 
I Edible oil; egg, fish & meat; milk and milk products; 
vegetables; fruits; spices; beverages and processed foods; 
pan, tobacco & intoxicants 
Last 7 days 
II All other food items; fuel and light; miscellaneous goods 
and services including medical (non -hospitalization) 
and rents 
Last 30 days 
III Clothing; bedding; footwear; education; medical 
(hospitalization); durable goods 
Last 365 days 
 
1.5.2. Together, these reference periods are termed as Modified Mixed Reference Period 
(MMRP). Other reference periods used in past NSS surveys on household consumption are: 
Uniform Reference Period (URP) and Mixed Reference Period (MRP).  
 
1.5.3 In URP approach, the household consumption expenditure on each item is recorded 
for a reference period of “ last 30 days” while in MRP , the household consumption 
expenditure on – (i) clothing and bedding, footwear, education, institutional medical care  
(i.e., on hospitalization)  and durable goods is recorded for a reference period of “last 365 
days”, and (ii) expenditure on all other items is recorded for a reference period of “last 30 
days”.  
 
1.6 HOUSEHOLD  
 
1.6.0 A group of persons normally living together and taking food from a common kitchen 
constitutes a household. It includes temporary stay -aways (with total period of absence 
from the household to be less than 6 months during the survey period) but excludes 
temporary visitors and guests (with total period of stay less than 6 months during the survey 
period).   

PDF_PAGE 24
 
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 6 
1.6.1 Each inmate of a hostel, mess, hotel, boarding and lodging house etc., is considered as 
a single member household except that a family living in a hotel (say) is considered as one 
household only; the same applies to the residential staff of such establishments.  Under -
trial prisoners in jails and indoor patients of hospitals, nursing homes, etc., are considered 
as members of the households to which they last belonged. 
 
1.7 HOUSEHOLD SIZE 
 
1.7.0 The size of a household is the total number of persons staying in the household. 
 
1.8 HOUSEHOLD CONSUMPTION EXPENITURE 
 
1.8.0 It is the expenditure incurred by a household on domestic consumption during the 
reference period. Expenditure incurred towards entrepreneurial activities of households is 
excluded from household consumption expenditure.   
 
1.8.1 The expenditure on purchase and construction of residential land and building, 
interest payments, insurance premium payments, payments of fines and penalties, and 
expenditure on gambling including lottery tickets are also excluded.  Money given as 
remittance, charity, gift, etc. is also not considered in consumption expenditure. However, 
consumption of items produced in own farm or household enterprise is valued and included 
in household consumption expenditure. Also, goods and services received as pa yment in 
kind or free from employer, such as rent -free accommodation and free medical care, and 
reimbursement of travelling allowance for self & family excluding allowance for business 
trips are considered in household consumption expenditure. 
 
1.8.2 For items of food, pan, tobacco, intoxicants and fuel & light, household consumption 
is measured by the quantity of the item actually used or consumed by the household during 
the reference period, regardless of the expenditure incurred on it.  For evaluating 
household consumption of all other items, a different approach is followed:  the 

PDF_PAGE 25
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023 -24                              Page 7  
expenditure made by the household during the reference period for the purchase or 
acquisition of goods and services, regardless of when the goods and services are used and 
by whom, is considered as household consumption.  
 
18.3. However, for some items such as rent, telephone charges, consumer taxes and railway 
season tickets, etc. expenditure during the month is recorded as the amount that was last 
paid divided by the number of months for which the payment was made. 
 
1.8.4 The consumption of an item may be out of (a) purchases made in cash or credit during 
the reference period; (b) home-grown stock; (c) receipts in exchange of goods and services; 
(d) any other receipt like gift, charity and (e) free collection. Home pr oduced items are 
evaluated at ex-farm price.  
 
1.8.5 It is pertinent to mention here that the consumption expenditure of a household on 
food items relates to the actual consumption by the members of the household and also for 
the guests during ceremonies or otherwise.  Any transfer payment made by the household 
such as, transfers of food, fuel made by a household as charity, loan advance etc. are not 
considered as consumption of that household.  However, meals prepared in a household 
and served to non-household members are an exception to this rule. Meals prepared in the 
household’s kitchen and provided to the employees and/or others are considered in 
domestic consumption of employer (payer) households.  There is a practical difficulty in 
estimating the quantities and values of individual items used for preparing the meals served 
to employees or others. Thus, to avoid double counting, ‘cooked meals received as 
perquisites’ from the employer household or as gifts or charity are not recorded in the 
recipient household. As a general principle, cooked meals purchased by the household from 
the market for consumption by the members and the guests are also recorded in the 
purchaser household.  
 

PDF_PAGE 26
 
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 8 
1.8.6 All goods and services received as payment in kind or perquisites are included in the 
consumption of the recipient household as goods and services received in exchange of 
services, except for meals received from other households.  
 
1.8.7 With a view to simplify data collection and valuation, consumption of food processed 
at home from one “item” to another, such as milk converted to curd, vegetables used for 
pickles, and rice used for making liquor etc. are recorded against the primary or ingredient 
item(s), such as milk instead of curd, vegetables instead of pickle etc.  
 
1.9 VALUE OF CONSUMPTION 
 
1.9.0 For food, pan, tobacco, intoxicants and fuel & light  items, consumption out of 
purchase is evaluated at the purchase price. Consumption out of home produce is evaluated 
at ex-farm price. Value of consumption out of gifts, free collections, and goods received in 
exchange of goods and services is imputed at the rate of average local retail prices prevailing 
during the reference period.  Such valuation is made by the field officials at the time of 
collection of the data. 
 
1.10 MONTHLY PER CAPITA CONSUMPTION 
EXPENDITURE (MPCE) 
 
1.10.0 The Monthly Per  Capita Consumption Expenditure ( MPCE) is defined as: total 
household monthly consumption expenditure divided by the household size. This measure 
serves as an indicator of the household’s level of living. 
 
1.10.1 Each individual’s MPCE is defined as the MPCE of the household to which the 
person belongs. This assigns to each person a number representing his or her level of living. 
Thus, the intra-household allocation among the members of the household is ignored.  
 
 

PDF_PAGE 27
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023 -24                              Page 9  
1.11 FRACTILES AND FRACTILE CLASSES OF MPCE 
 
1.11.0 For any fraction f (0<f<1), the corresponding fractile of the distribution of MPCE is 
the level of MPCE, Yf,  such that the proportion of population whose household MPCE lies 
below Yf  is f. A fractile class of MPCE is a segment of the population lying within two 
fractiles, f1 and f2.   
 
1.11.1 In this report, twelve fractile classes, represented as “0-5%”, “5-10%”, “10-20%”, …, 
“95-100%” are used.  Each of the first two classes contain 5% of the estimated population 
ranked by MPCE  in ascending order, i.e., the bottom 5% of the estimated population 
belongs to the class “0-5%”. Each of the next eight classes contain 10% of the estimated 
population ranked by MPCE in ascending order. The last two classes contain 5% each of 
the estimated population in which the fractile class “95-100%” represents top 5% of 
estimated population when ranked by MPCE in ascending order.  
 
1.11.2 Estimates are often generated separately , for different fractile classes to assess the 
variation of any characteristics with variation in MPCE. In this report, MPCE fractile 
classes, formed separately for different States/UTs and at all-India level for rural and urban 
sectors have been used.  
 
1.12 ISSUES RELATED TO COMPARABILITY 
 
1.12.0 HCES: 2022-23 has undergone some changes as compared to the previous surveys on 
consumption expenditure. A brief account of the changes made in this survey are given 
below. As a methodology identical to that of HCES:  2022-23 and the same set 
questionnaires (except for some modifications in Questionnaire HCQ) have been used in 
HCES: 2023-24, the following are required to be noted while comparing the results of 
HCES: 2023-24 with those of the surveys conducted in 2011-12 and earlier. 
 

PDF_PAGE 28
 
 
Chapter One                                                                                                                               Introduction  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 10 
(i) Item Coverage:  The questionnaire used in HCES:  2011-12 (NSS 68 th round) had 
around 347 items.  In HCES: 2022-23 & HCES: 2023-24, the questionnaire has been updated 
by inclusion of new items and merging some of the obsolete items together. The 
questionnaire of HCES: 2022-23 & HCES: 2023-24 contains 405 items.  
 
(ii) Changes in Questionnaire: A single questionnaire has been used in all NSS surveys 
on household consumption expenditure prior to HCES: 2022-23. However, in HCES: 2022-
23 & HCES: 2023-24, three separate questionnaires covering (i) food items, (ii) consumables 
and services items and (iii) durable goods have been used. Further, another questionnaire 
has been canvassed for collecting information on household characteristics as well as 
demographic particulars of the members of the households.  
 
(iii) Multiple Visits for Data Collection: Contrary to the usual practice of single visit 
followed prior to 2022 -23, three questionnaires, namely, FDQ, CSQ & DGQ have been 
canvassed at random in HCES: 2022-23 & HCES: 2023-24 in a selected household in three 
consecutive monthly visits in a quarter. The questionnaire HCQ has always been canvassed 
during the first visit along with one of these three questionnaires.   
 
(iv) Change in Mode of Data Collection: Computer-Assisted Personal Interview (CAPI) 
method has been adopted in HCES: 2022-23 & HCES: 2023-24 while all earlier surveys on 
the subject have been conducted in Pen-and-Paper Personal Interview (PAPI) method. 
 
1.13 MAJOR STATES 
 
1.13.0 This refers to the 18 States of India which had a population of 25 million or more 
according to the Census of 2011. The States are: Andhra Pradesh, Assam, Bihar, 
Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, Haryana, Jharkhand, Karnataka, Kerala, Madhya Pradesh, 
Maharashtra, Odisha, Punjab, Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh and West 
Bengal.

list of major states.  
 
1.14 CONTENTS OF THIS DOCUMENT 
 
1.14.0 This report is based on the Household Consumption Expenditure Survey conducted 
during August, 2023 to July, 2024. The report contains three Chapters with summary 
statements. This Chapter is the introductory one and provides a brief background of the  
survey in addition to elucidating some concepts and definitions used in the survey. Chapter 
2 outlines the survey methodology and estimation procedure while the findings of the 
survey are discussed in Chapter 3. The detailed tables of indicators, namely, MPCE, per 
capita quantity consumption etc. at State/UT level are included in Appendix A. The detailed 
estimation procedure is presented in Appendix B. As a measure of efficacy of the estimates, 
the values of relative standard errors (RSE) of the MPCE figures for each State/UT are given 
in Appendix C and the survey questionnaires are included in Appendix D.

Chapter Two 
Survey Method & Estimation of MPCE 
 
2.1 SAMPLING DESIGN 
 
2.1.0 A multistage stratified sampling design, considering villages/urban blocks or sub-units 
of these as the first stage units has been used in the survey. The households are the ultimate 
stage units. Simple Random Sampling Without Replacement (SRSWOR) method is used for 
selecting the samples.   
 
2.1.1 In order to ensure proper representation of households of different economic 
categories, all the households of a selected village/urban block are classified into three 
groups depending on a criterion based on (i) land possessed in rural areas and (ii) possession 
of car in urban areas as on the date of the survey. A total of 18 households with proportional 
representation from the three groups have been selected. The details of survey 
methodology and estimation procedure are provided in Appendix B.  
  
2.2 ESTIMATION OF MPCE 
 
2.2.0 The monthly per capita consumption expenditure (MPCE) of a household, defined as 
“the total value of monthly consumption of goods & services of the household divided by 
the number of members of the household ” is based on the data collected from those 
households who provided information for all the three visits, i.e., the households from 
whom data pertaining to all the three questionnaires, namely, FDQ, CSQ and DGQ are 
available. The multiplier computed from the data of third visit is used for generation of the 
estimates.  
 

PDF_PAGE 32
Chapter Two                                                                                       Survey Method & Estimation of MPCE  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023 -24                            Page 14  
2.2.1 It has been a practice in NSS household consumption expenditure surveys to impute 
the value figures at the time of collection of data in the field for consumption out of (i) 
home-grown/home-produced stock and (ii) gifts, free collection and goods received in 
exchange of goods and services etc. The same practice has been continued in HCES: 2022 -
23 & HCES:  2023-24 and accordingly, estimates of MPCE have been generated. These 
estimates are presented in Section I of Chapter 3.  
 
2.2.2 The National Statistical Commission suggested to make some provision in the survey 
questionnaires of HCES to collect information on items/services received free of cost by the 
households through various Social Welfare Schemes. Accordingly, a separate provision for 
collection of information on the quantity of consumption for a number of items, received 
and consumed by the households free of cost through various social welfare programmes , 
has been made in HCES: 2022-23 & HCES: 2023-24. Consequently, the value figures for (i) 
food items: Rice, Wheat/Atta, Jowar, Bajra, Maize, Ragi, Barley, Small Millets, Pulses, 
Gram, Salt, Sugar, Edible Oil and (ii) non -food items: Laptop/PC, Tablet, Mobile Handset, 
Bicycle, Motor Cycle/Scooty, Clothing (School uniform), Footwear (School shoe etc.) 
received free of cost by the households has been imputed using an appropriate method, in 
addition to the items as mentioned in para 2.2.1 above.  
 
2.2.3 Further, from January 2023, PDS items distributed to the NFSA beneficiaries have 
also been made free and consequently, the value figures of these items have also been 
imputed. Thereafter, as in HCES: 2022 -23, another set of estimates of MPCE has been 
generated for HCES: 202 3-24. These estimates, referred as ‘MPCE estimates with 
imputation’ are presented in Section II of Chapter 3.  
 
2.2.4 It may be noted that the benefits provided under PM-JAY or any other state specific 
schemes provide cashless access to healthcare services to the beneficiary at the point of 
delivery of service, i.e., hospital and the beneficiary does not have any information on the 
cost of the services availed. For such schemes, entire premium is borne by the Government 

PDF_PAGE 33
Chapter Two                                                                                              Survey Method & Esti mation of MPCE 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 15  
and the beneficiary does not contribute. Since HCES is not a record -based survey, often it 
is not possible to ascertain the exact ailment or disease for which the benefit has been 
availed. Hence, in view of the complexity and appropriateness involved in im putation of 
the expenditure for such services, no attempt has been made to impute the expenditure of 
health services availed by the households free of cost. For similar reasons, the expenditure 
for free education services (i.e., reimbursement/waiver of sch ool or college fees) has also 
not been imputed.

generated considering the imputed values of the following items , in addition to the items 
as mentioned in para 2.2.1 above:

PDF_PAGE 34
Chapter Two                                                                                       Survey Method & Estimation of MPCE  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023 -24                            Page 16  
2.3.1 The value figures of these free items have been imputed by identifying the unit price 
at which the beneficiary household would have bought the same if it is not received free 
of cost. While deciding on an appropriate method of imputation, it has been assum ed that 
all households belonging to a particular sector, state and second stage stratum (SSS) are 
homogeneous in terms of purchasing power and have more or less similar consumption 
pattern. Besides, consideration of a representative unit price for each of the imputed items 
at Sector × State × Second Stage Stratum (SSS) level would also address the variation in 
state-level prices. Thus, the adopted imputation method has been essentially based on 
finding an item -wise unit price at which the majority of the households would have 
purchased the item during the reference period. 
 
2.3.2 In the questionnaires of HCES, in addition to collection of information on quantity 
figures for items received free of cost, there is provision for gathering information on 
quantity and value of each of these items consumed by the households ‘out of purcha se’ 
during the reference period. From these quantity and value figures of each of the items 
consumed by the households ‘out of purchase’, item -level unit prices (value/quantity) are 
calculated for all households belonging to a particular Sector × State × Second Stage Stratum 
(SSS) level combination. Thereafter, the modal unit price for a particular item is determined 
at Sector × State × SSS level.  In the absence of availability of sufficient number of 
observations at Sector × State × SSS level, the modal unit values are determined at Sector × 
State level. Using these item -wise modal unit prices, the total value of consumption of a 
particular free item is imputed as:      
Imputed value = Quantity consumed × Modal unit value. 
 
2.3.3 For computation of Mode, Kernel Density Estimation procedure has been used i.e., 
each discrete point (i.e., each unit value) has been replaced by Gaussian distribution 
(kernel). The probability density at any given point in the space is then estimated to be the 
sum of the ke rnels at the chosen point over all the discrete points (after proper 
normalization). The rule-of-thumb bandwidth estimator has been used to approximate the 
width of a kernel or bandwidth. 

PDF_PAGE 35
Chapter Two                                                                                              Survey Method & Esti mation of MPCE 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 17  
2.3.4 In most of the cases while computing unit prices, it has been observed that sufficient 
observations are not present for at least one SSS (out of three) at Sector × State level. 
Therefore, to maintain uniformity, the unit prices for each item are calculated at the Sector 
× State level.

received free of cost are given in the tables below:

2.4.0 The efficacy of the estimates of average MPCE values for each State/UT has been 
measured in terms of Relative Standard Error (RSE), defined as: 
 
𝑹𝑺𝑬 =
𝑺𝒕𝒂𝒏𝒅𝒂𝒓𝒅 𝑬𝒓𝒓𝒐𝒓
𝑬𝒔𝒕𝒊𝒎𝒂𝒕𝒆 × 𝟏𝟎𝟎 %. 
2.4.1 The formula used for estimation of the RSE is provided in Appendix B. The estimated 
RSE values along with 95% confidence intervals for estimates of average MPCE figures are 
given in Appendix C.

Chapter Three 
Summary of Findings 
 
 
 
3.1 INTRODUCTION 
 
3.1.0 The prime indicator generated from the Household Consumption Expenditure Survey 
(HCES) is the average Monthly Per Capita Consumption Expenditure (MPCE), compiled at 
various level of disaggregation, namely, sector, State/UT or with  regard to various 
characteristics of the households like household type, social group, etc. In addition, share 
of expenditure incurred on consumption of different types of items, share in quantity of 
consumption of various food items are also compiled from  the information collected in 
HCES. Accordingly, discussions on the survey findings in this chapter are confined 
predominantly to these parameters. In addition, trend in MPCE covering past f ew NSS 
surveys on household consumption expenditure along with pattern in inequality in 
household consumption expenditure are also included in this Chapter.  
 
3.1.1 As discussed in para 2.2. 2 of Chapter 2, information on the quantity of consumption 
for a number of items, received and consumed by the households free of cost through 
various social welfare programmes has been collected in HCES: 2022 -23 and HCES: 2023-
24. Consequently, the value figures for (i) food items: Rice, Wheat/Atta, Jowar, Bajra, 
Maize, Ragi, Barley, Small Millets, Pulses, Gram, Salt, Sugar, Edible Oil and (ii) non -food 
items: Laptop/PC, Tablet, Mobile Handset, Bicycle, Motor Cycle/Scooty, Clothing (School 
uniform), Footwear (School shoe etc.) received free of cost by the households have been 
imputed using an appropriate method.  

PDF_PAGE 38
 
 
Chapter Three                                                                                                                     Summary of Findings 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 20 
3.1.2 As a consequence, two sets of estimates of MPCE have been generated from HCES: 
2023-24. The first one is by adopting the usual NSS practice of imputation of the value 
figures at the time of collection of data in the field for consumption out of home -
grown/home-produced stock and; gifts, free collection and goods received in exchange of 
goods and services, etc. These estimates are presented in Section I of the present Chapter.  
 
3.1.3 Another set of estimates of MPCE has been generated utilizing the imputed value 
figures of the items as mentioned in para 3.1.1 above in conjunction with adoption of usual 
NSS practice of imputation of values figures for the items by the field personnel as explained 
earlier. These estimates, referred as ‘MPCE estimates with imputation’ are presented in 
Section II of this Chapter.  
 
3.1.4 Since the ‘MPCE estimates with imputation’ are derived using the imputed values of 
the items as mentioned in para 3.1.1, the estimates of MPCE using the conventional NSS 
practice and ‘MPCE estimates with imputation’ will vary. Accordingly, for any domain or 
classification, the fractile classes of MPCE for the two types of MPCE figures will not be 
identical due to the fact that the set of households in the corresponding fractile classes will not, 
in general, be identical. Hence, any comparison of either MPCE values or share of expenditure 
between the two sets of MPCE estimates across the fractile classes to assess the impact of social 
welfare schemes will not be meaningful. Consequently, estimates of MPCE values with 
imputation as well as various types of cross -classifications of the same by fractile classes of 
MPCE have not been generated.    
 
3.1.5 Further, the fractile classes for rural and urban areas have been determined separately.  
Similarly, state-specific fractile classes are also derived and state -wise estimates for these 
classes are given in the detailed tables in Appendix A.  
 
3.1.6 Apart from the average MPCE, the survey data has also been used to generate 
estimates of various parameters, separately for households for different fractile classes of 
MPCE. Among these, the absolute and percentage break -up of MPCE over  categories of 

PDF_PAGE 39
 
Chapter Three                                                                                                                     Summary of Findings
  
 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                                 Page 21  
consumption items, and quantity and value of per capita consumption of different cereals 
are important indicators.  
 
3.1.7 The discussions in this chapter covers the following for rural and urban sectors: 
 
(a) average MPCE and MPCE across fractile classes, 
(b) trend in level of consumption,  
(c) median and deciles of the distribution of population by MPCE, 
(d) average MPCE across household size, 
(e) share of various food and non-food item groups in total expenditure,  
(f) inter-state variation in food and cereal consumption, 
(g) trend in per capita quantity consumption of cereals, 
(h) inter-state variation of expenditure for different food and non-food items,  
(i) trend in pattern of consumption of broad item groups, 
(j) average MPCE across social groups, 
(k) average MPCE across household types, 
(l) inequality in distribution of MPCE, 
(m) comparison of two types of estimates of MPCE figures.

NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 22 
3.2 SECTION I: ESTIMATES OF MPCE 
 
3.2.0 The estimates presented in this section have been generated without imputing the 
value figures of the items (as mentioned in Para 3.1.1) received free by the households 
through various social welfare programmes. The value figures shown in tables and 
statements throughout this chapter are expressed as nominal values, unless otherwise stated 
and represents expenditure in Indian Rupee as reported by the respondents. 
 
3.2.1 AVERAGE MPCE: MAJOR STATES AND ALL-INDIA 
 
3.2.1.0 The average MPCE in rural and urban India in 2023-24 has been estimated to be Rs. 
4,122 and Rs. 6,996, respectively without considering the values of items received free of 
cost through various social welfare programmes (Table 3.1). Thus, the per c apita 
expenditure level in the urban areas, in nominal terms, and ignoring rural-urban differences 
in price levels, has been about 70% more than that in the rural areas.

Note: (i) For the years 1999-‘00 & 2004-05, estimates are based on Mixed Reference Period (MRP) and for the 
years 2009-10, 2011-12, 2022-23 and 2023-24, these are based on Modified MRP (MMRP). 
MRP: The estimate of MPCE is based on household consumption expenditure on – (i) clothing and bedding, 
footwear, education, institutional medical care, and durable goods recorded for a reference period of “last 365 
days”, and (ii) expenditure on all other items recorded for a reference period of “last 30 days”.  
MMRP: The estimate of MPCE is based on household consumption expenditure on edible oil, egg, fish and 
meat, vegetables, fruits, spices, beverages, refreshments, processed food, pan, tobacco and intoxicants recorded 
for a reference period of “last 7 days”, and f or all other items, recorded for the same reference period as in case 
of Mixed Reference Period (except for milk & milk products for which 7 -days reference period has been 
considered in HCES:2022-23). 
(ii) For the years 1999 -‘00, 2004 -05 and 2009 -10 the rural and urban MPCE estimates have been inflated 
using CPI(AL) and CPI(IW -General) respectively, to obtain the MPCE values at 2011 -12 price. The MPCE 
estimates of 2022 -23 and 2023 -24 have been deflated using CPI(R) & CPI(U) for rural and urban sectors 
respectively to obtain the MPCE values at 2011-12 price.

3.2.3 MEDIAN AND DECILES OF THE MPCE DISTRIBUTION:

3.2.4 MPCE AND HOUSEHOLD SIZE: ALL-INDIA 
 
3.2.4.0 Figure 3 shows MPCE values for 10 different household sizes. A steady decline in 
MPCE figures is observed with an increase in the size of the household. The MPCE figures 
for single member households have been, on an average, the highest while it is observed to 
be the lowest for households with 10 or more members. The same scenario is found to be 
present in rural as well as in urban areas.

followed by milk & milk products (8. 44%) and vegetables ( 6.03%) in rural India. The 
contribution of cereals and cereal substitutes in the expenditure has been about 4.99%.

3.2.5.4 While interpreting observations from Table 3.5 above, the following needs to be 
considered: 
 
3.2.5.5 All averages are computed taking the entire estimated population in the           
denominator. This fact may be borne in mind, specially, for those items showing very low 
average expenditure, like ‘rent’ and ‘taxes and cesses’ in rural India.  The actual number of 
consuming persons in the population is not considered here for any of the item groups. For 
some item groups such as cereals, consuming persons would mean nearly the entire 
population; for other items, such as tobacco, it would obviously be much smaller.  
 
 3.2.5.6 “Expenditure” on food includes the value of self -consumed home produce, 
calculated at ex -farm prices. It also includes the value of food collected free, for             
example, from forests, imputed at prevailing local retail prices. “Expenditure” on fuel, 
likewise, includes the value of home-grown firewood, calculated at ex-farm prices, and the 
value of firewood and other fuel collected free, imputed at local retail prices. 

PDF_PAGE 50
 
 
Chapter Three                                                                                                                     Summary of Findings 
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24                           Page 32 
3.2.5.7 “Processed food” refers to purchased food items such as jam, pickles, etc., the 
quantities and values of the individual ingredients of which are not known well enough to 
be reported against the detailed items listed in the consumer expenditure schedule. All food 
processed at home is accounted against the ingredients. For example, a sweet prepared at 
home and consumed is accounted in the household’s (and the concerned State/UTs) 
consumption of cereals, sugar, milk, etc., but not in its consumption of  processed food. 
Cooked meals and snacks purchased from outside are also considered in this category. 
 
3.2.5.8 Spending on tailor -made clothing does not include tailoring charges, which are 
included in “consumer services”. 
 
3.2.5.9 Figure 4 below shows the percentage share of broad item groups in total MPCE for 
rural and urban sector.

3.2.9.0 Tables 3.9R and 3.9U below elucidate inter -state variation in food consumption 
pattern, with regard to contribution of cereals, processed food & beverages, milk & milk 
products, egg, fish & meat, vegetables and fruits to total food MPCE, separately for rural 
and urban sectors. 
 

PDF_PAGE 55

3.2.9.1 In rural sector, the share of the item category ‘beverages, processed food etc.’ in total 
food consumption has been maximum in 9 out of 18 major states. While the share of ‘milk 
and milk products’ has been the maximum in Gujarat, Haryana, Punjab, Rajasthan and 
Uttar Pradesh, in Kerala, ‘egg, fish & meat’ has the highest contribution.  In urban sector 
also, the share of the item group ‘beverages, processed food etc.’ has been maximum in total 
food expenditure for almost all major states except for Chattisgarh, Haryana, Madhya 
Pradesh, Rajasthan and Uttar Pradesh where maximum share of consumption has been for 
milk & milk products.

3.2.11 TREND IN PATTERN OF CONSUMPTION OF BROAD 
ITEM GROUPS: ALL-INDIA 
 
3.2.11.0 Tables 3.11R and 3.11U show changes in percentage composition of MPCE in terms 
of broad item groups as shown by the past five NSS surveys beginning with 1999 -00 
separately, for rural and urban sectors. The share of food is seen to have shrunk from 
59.40% in 1999-00 to 47.04% in 2023-24 in the rural areas and from 48. 06% to 39.68% in 
the urban areas during the corresponding period.  
 
3.2.11.1 Although, there has been a decline in the share of food items like cereals, pulses & 
pulse products and sugar & salt over the period, the item category of “processed food, 
beverages, etc.” has shown a remarkable growth in contribution to total MPCE in 2023-24 
when compared with the earlier periods. The decline in the share has been the steepest for 
cereals which has come down to 4.89% in 2022-23 from 22.16% in 1999-‘00 in rural India 
and for urban India, the corresponding share has declined to 3.62% in 2022-23 from 12.35% 
in 1999-‘00. Similar trend in consumption of cereals continues in 2023-24.

3.2.13 AVERAGE MPCE ACROSS HOUSEHOLD TYPES: MAJOR 
STATES 
 
3.2.13.0 Tables 3.13R and 3.13U present the average MPCE (rural and urban) by     
household type for each major state. It is observed that the households belonging to the 
‘regular wage/salary earning in non-agriculture’ has the highest average MPCE (Rs. 5,005), 
followed by households with ‘others’ category (Rs. 4, 747) in rural India. The rural 
households belonging to the category ‘casual labour in agriculture’ has the lowest MPCE 
(Rs. 3, 652). In urban areas, the households of ‘others’ category has the highe st average 
MPCE (Rs. 9,159) while it is the lowest for households of ‘casual labour’ category (Rs. 
4,964).

3.3 SECTION II: ESTIMATES OF MPCE - WITH                          
IMPUTATION 
 
3.3.0 The estimates of average MPCE presented here have been generated considering the 
imputed value figures of the items received free by the households through various social 
welfare programmes (as mentioned in para 3.1.1), in addition to the imputed val ues of 
consumption out of home produce, free collection, gifts etc. as explained in para 2.2.2 of 
Chapter 2. With a view to reduce redundancy and lessen burden of the readers, this section 
is not made an identical replica of Section I in terms of compositi on of sub -sections and 
contents thereof. Instead, in view of the fact that imputation is restricted to few selected 
items only, changes in the estimates of average MPCE figures by various cross -
classifications with regard to different domains are presented in summarized tabular format 
along with a comparison with the MPCE figures discussed in Section I.  
 
3.3.1 A separate data file containing the modal unit prices used for imputation of the value 
figures for (i) food items: Rice, Wheat/Atta, Jowar, Bajra, Maize, Ragi, Barley, Small 
Millets, Pulses, Gram, Salt, Sugar, Edible Oil and (ii) non -food items: Laptop/PC, Tablet, 
Mobile Handset, Bicycle, Motor Cycle/Scooty, Clothing (School uniform), Footwear 
(School shoe etc.) received free of cost by the households is being released with the unit 
level data. This would allow the users to generate estimates of MPCE and its various 
components with imputation at disaggregated levels.

Note: (i) For the years 1999-‘00 & 2004-05, estimates are based on Mixed Reference Period (MRP) and for the years 2009 -10, 2011-12 
and 2022-23, these are based on Modified MRP (MMRP). 
MRP: The estimate of MPCE is based on household consumption expenditure on – (i) clothing and bedding, footwear, education, 
institutional medical care, and durable goods recorded for a reference period of “last 365 days”, and (ii) expenditure on all  other items 
recorded for a reference period of “last 30 days”. 
MMRP: The estimate of MPCE is based on household consumption expenditure on edible oil, egg, fish and meat, vegetables, fruits, spices, 
beverages, refreshments, processed food, pan, tobacco and intoxicants recorded for a reference period of “last 7 days”, and f or all other 
items, recorded for the same reference period as in case of Mixed Reference Period (except for milk & milk products for which  7-days 
reference period has been considered in HCES:2022-23). 
(ii) For the years 1999-‘00, 2004-05 and 2009-10 the rural and urban MPCE estimates have been inflated using CPI(AL) and CPI(IW -
General) respectively, to obtain the MPCE values at 2011-12 price. The MPCE estimates of 2022-23 have been deflated using CPI(R) & 
CPI(U) for rural and urban sectors respectively to obtain the MPCE values at 2011-12 price.

A Note for the Users of Unit Level Data of HCES: 2023-24 
 
The objective of the Household Consumption Expenditure Survey (HCES): 2023 -24 has been to 
collect information on consumption of goods and services by the households for  generation of 
indicators for understanding the consumption and expenditure pattern, standard of living and 
well-being of the households. The main indicator compiled on the basis of the data collected 
through HCES is the Monthly Per Capita Consumption Expenditure (MPCE) of the household. 
 
Like all other NSS surveys, in this survey too, there has been provision to collect information on 
household characteristics e.g., social group, religion, household type, etc.  along with                       
demographic particulars of household members, namely, age, marital status, highest              
educational level attained, etc. in Section 3 and Section 4 of the questionnaire HCQ.                  
Information on these ite ms of Sections 3 & 4 have been recorded by the survey enumerators on 
the basis o f reporting by the respondents and no cross -check/validation of these has been done 
with the relevant documents. The purpose of gathering information on these auxiliary variables 
has been to generate estimates of the indicators related to the variables of interest by different 
types cross-classifications and not to generate any estimates like sex ratio, literacy rates, percentage 
of population by various social groups etc. on the basis of these auxiliary information. 
Consequently, data related to these auxi liary variables were recorded as reported by the 
respondent and not verified with any administrative record(s). Hence, the users of the unit level 
data are advised not to make use of the auxiliary information for estimation of indicators, as such 
an exercise would result in misleading and inappropriate conclusions.      
     
The survey has also gathered auxiliary information on the following items: 
• Online purchase/payment of some selected goods and services, 
• Household’s usual consumption expenditure in a month,  
• Total expenditure incurred on online purchase/payment of food items/consumable &  
services items during the last 30 days and expenditure incurred on online             
purchase/payment of durable goods during the last 365 days etc. 
The sole purpose of gathering on these items has been to check internal consistency and               
validation of the data and not to compile estimates/indicators based on this information. 
States/UTs have been regarded as the basic stratum in HCES: 202 3-24. Thus, any attempt to 
generate estimates of MPCE below State/UT level may lead to inaccurate/inappropriate    figures 
in the absence of availability of sufficient number of sample observations at district level and users 
should refrain from generating such estimates.

NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24           Page B1 
Survey Methodology & Estimation Procedure 
 
 
 
 
2.1 INTRODUCTION 
 
2.1.0 The latest survey on household consumption expenditure (previously known as 
household consumer expenditure survey) was conducted during the period August 2023 
to July 2024 in which information was collected from each sampled household in three 
questionnaires, namely, Questionnaire: FDQ (Food Items), Questionnaire: CSQ 
(Consumables & Services) and Questionnaire: DGQ (Durable Items) in three separate 
monthly visits in a quarter. Apart from these, another questionnaire, namely, 
Questionnaire: HCQ was canvassed to collect information on household characteristics.  
 
2.2 SAMPLING DESIGN 
 
2.2.0 In HCES: 202 3-24, a multi -stage stratified sampling design was used where 
villages/urban blocks or sub -units of these were regarded as the First Stage Units (FSU) 
and the households were the Ultimate Stage Units (USU).  Both the FSUs and USUs were 
selected with Simple Random Sampling Without Replacement (SRSWOR).  18 sample 
households were canvassed within an FSU.  
 
2.2.1 CONCEPT OF PANEL 
 
2.2.1.0 The survey period of HCES: 2023-24 was divided into 10 panels, each consisting 
of three months. The compositions of the panels were as below: 
 
 
 

PDF_PAGE 154
Appendix B                                                               Survey Methodology & Estimation Procedure  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24           Page B2 
Panel 
number 
Time period 
Panel 
number 
Time period 
Panel 1 August - October 2023 Panel 6 January - March 2024 
Panel 2 September – November 2023 Panel 7 February - April 2024 
Panel 3 October - December 2023 Panel 8 March - May 2024 
Panel 4 November 2023- January 2024 Panel 9 April - June 2024 
Panel 5 December 2023- February 2024 Panel 10 May - July 2024 
 
2.2.1.1 In each of these panels, an equal number of FSUs were allotted for survey to ensure 
uniform spread of the sample over the entire survey period. Because of arduous field 
conditions, panel restriction was not strictly  enforced in the Andaman and Nicobar 
Islands, Lakshadweep, Ladakh and rural areas of Arunachal Pradesh and Nagaland.  
 
2.2.1.2 In the first month of any panel , Questionnaire: HCQ along with any one of the 
questionnaires, i.e., FDQ/CSQ/DGQ were canvassed in the selected households. During 
the second month  of the panel , any one f rom the remaining two questionnaires was 
canvassed and in the last month , the last questionnaire was canvassed. The sequence of 
the questionnaires to be canvassed  in each month of a panel for a particular FSU was 
decided randomly to eliminate bias that may arise due to the adoption of a particular 
sequencing for canvassing. Thus, all six possible sequences, i.e., [(Q1, Q2, Q3), (Q1, Q3, 
Q2), (Q2, Q1, Q3), (Q2, Q3, Q1), (Q3, Q1, Q2) and (Q3, Q2, Q1)], where Q1 refers to 
FDQ, Q2 refers to CSQ and Q3 refers to DGQ, were canvassed at random in the sample 
households.  
 
2.2.2 CHOICE OF FIRST STAGE UNIT / SAMPLING FRAME 
FOR FIRST STAGE UNIT 
 
2.2.2.0 The sampling frame for urban sector is the list of Urban Frame Survey (UFS) 
blocks as per latest Urban Frame Survey and for rural sector, it is the list of villages as per 

PDF_PAGE 155
Appendix B                                                               Survey Methodology & Estimation Procedure  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24           Page B3 
Census 2011 updated by removing those villages which are urbanized and included in 
latest UFS (till the time of sample selection). Sometimes, with a view to ensure uniformity 
in the size of FSUs and operational convenience, large villages/UFS blocks are notionally 
divided into smaller units of more or less equal size, known as sub -units depending on a 
pre-defined criteria based on population in the village or number of households in the 
UFS block. The sector-specific criteria for sub-unit formation are as below:  
 
Rural Sector 
(i) The number of SUs to be formed in the villages (with Census 2011 population of 1000 
or more  and except some States/UTs ) are decided based on projected present 
population of the village. The criteria are given below: 
 
Projected Population of the village Number of SUs to be formed 
less than 1200 1 
1200 to 2399 2 
2400 to 3599 3 
… … 
 
(ii)  For rural areas of Himachal Pradesh, Sikkim, Andaman & Nicobar Islands, Ladakh, 
Parts of Uttarakhand (except four districts Dehradun, Nainital, Haridwar and Udham 
Singh Nagar), Jammu and Kashmir (seven districts Poonch, Rajouri, Udhampur, Reasi, 
Doda, Kishtwar, Ramban)  and Idukki district of Kerala ; SU is formed in a village if 
population as per Census 2011 is more than or equals to  500. The criteria for  the 
number of SU to be formed are as below: 
 
Projected Population of the village Number of SUs to be formed 
less than 600 1 
600 to 1199 2 
1200 to 1799 3 
… … 
 

PDF_PAGE 156
Appendix B                                                               Survey Methodology & Estimation Procedure  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24           Page B4 
Urban Sector:  
(i) SUs are formed in those UFS blocks having more than or equal to 250 households. 
The number of SUs to be formed within the UFS blocks is decided by the following 
criteria: 
Number of Households in UFS Block Number of SUs to be formed 
less than 250 1 
250 to 499 2 
500 to 749 3 
… … 
 
Thus, the list of Villages / UFS Blocks / Sub-Units (for those villages or UFS blocks where 
sub-units are formed within) together formed the sampling frame for First Stage Unit 
selection. 
 
2. 2.3 STRATIFICATION OF FSUS 
 
2.2.3.0 A State/UT is considered as a basic geographical unit for stratum formation in both 
the sectors for HCES:2023-24. The sector-specific stratification criteria are given below: 
 
Rural Sector:  
(i) A Special Stratum comprising of all the uninhabited villages as per Census 2011 is 
formed at All-India level. 
 
(ii) From the remaining villages, two more strata are formed in each State/UT: 
 
(a) Stratum 1: Comprising of the villages within a distance of 5 Kms from the district 
headquarter or from a city/town with more than 5 lakh population . This stratum 
is not formed if there are less than 50 such villages in the State/UT.  
 
(b) Stratum 2: Rest of the villages.

Appendix B                                                               Survey Methodology & Estimation Procedure  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24           Page B5 
Urban Sector: UFS blocks within each State/UT are divided into strata based on 
two criteria i.e., Population as per Census 2011 and ‘Affluence’ status as indicated in UFS 
frame. The stratum compositions are as follows:

special rural stratum at all-India level based on the following criteria:

2.2.4.1 Further, the sample size for a particular rural stratum is distributed among these 
3 groups in proportion to population and if the group-wise allocation is more than 20, 2 
or more sub-strata are formed in each group. If the number of FSUs in a particular group 
is very small, no sub-stratum is formed.   
 
2.2.4.2 In the urban sector, if the sample size allocated for an urban stratum is more than 
20, 2 or more sub-strata are formed based on the number of FSUs within that stratum. 
 
2.2.5 SAMPLE SIZE  
 
2.2.5.0 8,684 FSUs in the rural areas and 6,143 in the urban areas have been surveyed in 
HCES:2023-24. The total sample size of 14,827 FSUs has been allocated to State/UTs in 
proportion to Census 2011 population, subject to a minimum allocation of 40 FSUs.  
 
2.2.6 SELECTION OF FSUS WITHIN A STRATUM/SUB -
STRATUM  
 
2.2.6.0 The required number of FSUs from each of the sub-strata is selected by SRSWOR 
scheme. The selected sample is divided into 10 Panels with 2 dependent s ub-samples 
within each Panel .  Three sequences of questionnaires are canvassed in the FSUs 
belonging to sub -sample 1 and remaining three sequences are canvassed in FSUs sub-
sample 2. 
 
2.2.7 FORMATION OF SUB-DIVISION  
 
2.2.7.0 It has been experienced that in some of the selected FSU s, the actual present 
population is significantly higher than the projected population/Census population  that 
causes operational inconvenience for listing of all the households. In such a situation, the 
selected FSU is notionally sub-divided into several smaller units, called Sub-division. The

Sub-divisions. Further, listing and selection of households are done in the selected Sub-
division unit only.

second stage stratum (SSS) with the following criteria:

3.5 ESTIMATION OF MONTHLY PER CAPITA CONSUMER 
EXPENDITURE (MPCE) FROM THREE QUESTIONNAIRES:  
 
3.5.0 The monthly per capita consumption expenditure (MPCE) of a household, 
defined as “the total value of monthly consumption of goods & services of 
the household divided by the number of members of the household ” is 
based on the data collected from those households who provided information for all 
the three visits, i.e., the households from whom data pertaining to all the three 
questionnaires, namely, FDQ, CSQ and DGQ are available. The multiplier computed 
from the data of third visit  is used for generation of the estimates.  Household size 
recorded at the time of canvassing of food questionnaire (FDQ) is considered for 
calculating MPCE.  
 
3.5.1 Let E1, E2 & E3 be the total value of expenditure on food, consumable & services 
and durables items respectively for a particular sampled household, compiled from 
the three questionnaires, namely FDQ, CSQ & DGQ. Further, assume that P1, P2 & 
P3 be the number of household members (i.e., size of the hous ehold) recorded 
during the time of canvassing FDQ, CSQ and DGQ questionnaire, respectively.  
 
3.5.2 Then the total monthly expenditure of the household, say, TE is computed as: 
TE = E1 + (E2/P2) ×P1 + (E3/P3) ×P1 
 

PDF_PAGE 164
Appendix B                                                               Survey Methodology & Estimation Procedure  
NSS Report no. 592: Survey on Household Consumption Expenditure: 2023-24           Page B12 
3.5.3 Consequently, the MPCE of the household is computed as: 
MPCE = TE/P1.

Section 4: Household characteristics 
Question 
no. 
Question description and code structure  Recorded entry 
Q4.1 Whether any household member (excluding those employed by the household 
and paying guests) was engaged in economic activities during last 365 days?  
o yes-1 
o no-2 
 
If code is 1 in Q4.1 fill up Q4.2 to Q4.6 else go to Q4.10 
(Instruction:  
Economic activity includes: 
(i) all the market activities, i.e., the activities performed for pay or profit 
which result in production of goods and services for exchange, 
(ii) of the non-market activities, 
 
(a) all the activities relating to the primary sector (i.e., industry Divisions 
01 to 09 of NIC-2008) which result in production (including free collection 
of uncultivated crops, forestry, firewood, hunting, fishing, mining, 
quarrying, etc.) of primary goods, including threshing and storing of grains 
for own consumption.  
and 
(b) the activities relating to the own -account production of fixed assets. 
Own account production of fixed assets includes construction of own 
houses, roads, wells, etc. and of machinery, tools, etc. for household 
enterprise and also construction of any private or community facilities free 
of charge. ) 
 
Q4.2 Write the d escription of the occupation of the economic activity of the 
household members which fetched the maximum earnings to the household 
during the last 365 days preceding the date of survey 
[Instruction: List all the occupations pertaining to economic activities 
pursued by the members. Out of the occupations listed that one which fetched 
the maximum earnings to the household during the last 365 days preceding 
the date of survey would be considered as the pr incipal household 
occupation.] 
Text Box 
Q4.3 Record the 3 -digit code of the occupation (as per National Classification of 
Occupation (NCO 2015)) recorded in Q4.2 
DDM (3-digit code 
of NCO 2015) 
Q4.4 Write the d escription of the industry of activity corresponding to the 
occupation recorded in Q 4.2, which fetched the maximum earnings  to the 
household during the last 365 days preceding the date of survey 
[Instruction:  
• If corresponding to the principal occupation there is only one 
industry of activity, record the description of that industry of activity. 
• If the principal occupation is pursued in different industries, the 
particular industry out of all the different industries corresponding 
to the principal occupation, which fetched the maximum earnings, 
should be considered as the principal industry of the household.] 
 
Q4.5 Record the 5-digit code of the National Industrial Classification (NIC -2008) 
for the industry of activity recorded in Q4.4 
DDM (5-digit code 
of NIC 2008)

Section 4: Household characteristics 
Question 
no. 
Question description and code structure  Recorded entry 
o regular wage/salary earning -2 
o casual labour -3 
[Instruction: for urban samples, go to Q4.10 
For rural samples,  
• if 1 in Q4.6, go to Q4.7 
• if 2 in Q4.6, go to Q4.8 
• if 3 in Q4.6, go to Q4.9] 
(Note:  
self-employment: Persons who operate their own farm or non -farm 
enterprises or are engaged independently in a profession or trade on own -
account or with one or a few partners are deemed to be self -employed in 
household enterprises. 
regular wage/salary earning: Persons working in other’s farm or non -farm 
enterprises (both household and non-household) and getting in return salary 
or wages on a regular basis (and not on the basis of daily or periodic renewal 
of work contract) are the regular wage/salaried employees. 
casual labour: A person casually engaged in other’s farm or non -farm 
enterprises (both household and non -household including in public works) 
and getting in return wage according to the terms of the daily or periodic 
work contract is a casual wage labour. ) 
Q4.7 Whether the major income from self -employment was from agricultural 
sector or non-agricultural sector? 
o self-employment in agriculture -1 
o self-employment in non-agriculture-2 
 
Q4.8 Whether the major income from regular wage/ salary earning was from 
agricultural sector or non-agricultural sector? 
o regular wage/salary earning in agriculture-3 
o regular wage/salary earning in non-agriculture-4 
 
Q4.9 Whether the major income from casual labour was from agricultural sector or 
non-agricultural sector? 
o casual labour in agriculture -5 
o casual labour in non-agriculture-6

improvisations built by the roadside (which are liable to be removed at any

purchase and home-grown stock-3, only free collection-4, only exchange of

gas (343), (iii) coal (337), (iv) dung cake  (333), (v) petrol (litre) [excl.

natural gas (CNG, etc.) (340), (viii) matches (box) (336), (ix) candle (no.) 
(342) and (x) others (346). 
5. ^ 1 cubic meter=0.68 Kg 
6.’®’To find out the value of consumption of LPG during the reference 
period, the number of days normally taken by the household to use up one 
full cylinder of gas is to be first ascertained. Suppose the price of a cylinder 
of gas is Rs. ‘V’ and the household usually consumes one full cylinder in 
'D' days. Then the value of gas consumed during the last 30 days will be 
calculated as (V × 30)/D and rounded off to the nearest whole number of 
rupees]
